 When coupling FAST to MOOSE, MOOSE doesn’t use the information that it is passed directly.
 MOOSE works as a middleman to pass the information it receives to another software like TRACE.
 The same thing is true about the information MOOSE sends to FAST. MOOSE does not calculate
 the values listed in the MOOSE-to-FAST transfers, but obtains it from a software like TRACE and
 sends it to FAST.

 5.3.1 Renodalization of FAST with MOOSE

 The MOOSE interface in FAST includes the ability to renodalize FAST. As described previously,
 one of the parameters the MOOSE interface can receive is the cell lengths for the axial nodes,
 fine_cell_length. When the size of the array does not match the size of the current array for the
 axial node lengths (deltaz in the MOOSE interface), a call to the subroutine setupFineMesh is

External Code Interfaces 172

 made. This subroutine takes in the two arrays holding the cell lengths and uses the differences in
 lengths and size of the array to linearly interpolate all of the arrays that store axial information in
 FAST.

 5.4 Output File for FAST-to-TRACE Data Transfer
 For design basis accidents, the NRC uses the system-level thermal-hydraulics code TRACE for
 assessing the figures of merit for fuel behavior (which are typically burst, peak cladding temper-
 ature (PCT) and ECR. TRACE has simplified fuel performance models that are based on those
 found in fast (i.e., models that impact temperature distribution). However, TRACE is designed to
 work for short duration transients and therefore can not calculate the burnup of the fuel rod. FAST
 is used to provide the burnup-dependent information to TRACE to ensure that fuel temperatures
 and stored energy are not underestimated. In addition, this information is used to support the pro-
 posed new NRC LOCA regulations in 10 CFR 50.46(c) that require LOCA analyses to account for
 the pre-existing hydrogen in the cladding, which impacts how much transient oxidation can occur
 within the regulatory limits.

 To perform this data transfer, the NRC uses the SNAP interface to transfer the data from FAST
 outputs into a TRACE input file to develop fuel rod inputs for both PWRs and BWRs, which are
 referred to in TRACE as heatstructures and CHANs, respectively. The NRC also uses a tool called
 MAKECHAN, developed under NRC contract by Energy Research, Inc., (ERI) which works with
 PARCS outputs to develop a TRACE/PARCS input deck.

 This section outlines the file format that has been agreed upon by NRC, ERI and APT that contains
 all of the necessary information from a FAST calculation to populate a TRACE “fuel rod”. To turn
 on the writing of this file, see Section B.3.

 5.4.1 File Variables

 The following burnup-dependent information calculated by FAST is needed to populate the fuel
 rod input parameters in TRACE:

 • Gas composition - Total moles, mole fraction of each species, pressure

 • Fuel composition - Axial Gadolinia content

 • Burnup - Rod average, axial, and 2D (axial/radial)

 • Deformations - Fuel swelling, densification and cladding inner radius permanent deformation

 • Cladding data - Hydrogen content, oxide layer thickness, crud thickness

 • Mesh data - Meshpoints, power and temperatures

 Through sensitivity studies, the NRC has decided to use the number of moles of gas to calculate
 the rod internal pressure rather than the rod internal pressure input option. There are several
 reasons for this, with the most significant (as of TRACE V5P5) being that the rod internal pressure

External Code Interfaces 173

 input for TRACE is assumed to be taken at room temperature, whereas the value produced by
 FAST is at operating conditions. It is not sufficient to assume a simple ideal gas law (P V = nRT )
 due to the changes in void volume associated with the change in fuel temperature.

 5.4.2 File Units

 The file is output entirely in SI, regardless of the FAST input file units (which can be SI or British).
 The units for each variable are shown in Table 5-3.

 Appendix A – Running the Code
 The FAST code is designed to be run using a command prompt on Windows and Linux based
 operating systems, but the code can also be run through a graphical user interface. The NRC
 currently uses and supports the Symbolic Nuclear Analysis Package (SNAP) that can serve as
 the GUI to create the input file, run the code, and visualize the results. SNAP can also be used
 to link FAST with other NRC supported codes, such as the thermal-hydraulics code TRACE, and
 for performing statistical analysis using DAKOTA. For more information about SNAP, visit https:
 //www.aptplot.com.

 Using a command-line interface, the only (optional) argument that is allowed is the input file name.
 By default, the input file that the FAST executable looks for is 7biXBM.

 A.1 Windows-Based OS
 On a Windows based computer, by double clicking on the executable the code will automatically
 try to run using the file 7biXBM. If the file does not exist, the code will prompt the user to re-enter
 the file name, at which point the user can type in the file name they wish to use. It is important
 that the input file be in the same directory as the executable. When using a Windows command
 prompt, use the +/ command to move into the working directory (the folder which contains the
 executable and the input file), type in the name of the executable followed by a space and the
 name of the input file (7biX2t2 I6AG1LJ1=). An example is shown in Figure A.1.

 Figure A.1. Running FAST using the Windows Command Prompt

 A.2 Linux-Based OS
 Running FAST on a Linux based OS is nearly identical to running on Windows OS. Rather than
 typing in the name of the program (7bi.2t2), type in “Xf” followed by the name of the program
 (Xf7bi IALSlh 6AG1LJ11=). An example is shown in Figure A.2.

Running the Code A.1

 Figure A.2. Running FAST using the Linux Command Line (OS=Ubuntu 16)

 A.3 Using SNAP
 Once a SNAP model has been created (or imported from an ASCII file), go to hQQHb@@= am#KBi
 CQ#ē and the job will be submitted to the working directory specified in the model.

Running the Code A.2

 Appendix B – Input Instructions for the FAST-1.2.2 Code
 This appendix contains the requirements for the input file structure and the available input param-
 eters for FAST-1.2.2.

 B.1 File and Comment Structure
 The input file contains the information for fuel rod design, model options, defining the mesh, reactor
 conditions, and code output options. The beginning of the file is designated for naming output files
 and adding any comments.

 Any comments to be included at the top of the input file should start on Line 1 with a “ ”. Up
 to 200 characters are allowed on these lines, and as many lines as needed are allowed. It is
 highly recommended that the version of FAST the input file was developed for be included in
 these comments.

 After all comments are completed, the input and output file specification begins. The list of allowed
 input files and available output files is shown In Table B.1. Each input file requires the following
 information: 6AG1O4^6BH2LK2X1ti2MbBQM^-ahhla4^aiimbhvT2^

 Where O and 1ti2MbBQM are shown in Table 5.1, 6BH2LK2 is defined by the user (recommended
 to be the same as the name of the input file) and aiimbhvT2 is “UNKNOWN” for Output files and
 “OLD” for Input Files.

 Table B.1. Available input and output files in FAST-1.2.2

 B.8 bT2Mi7m2H Block
 This block contains the information related to the performing spent fuel calculations. These calcu-
 lations are performed after the in-reactor analysis is completed.

 In FAST, there are two ways to model the time as spent fuel. The first option is to use the DATING
 model which is a standalone model initialized using parameters from the FAST calculation. If this
 option is selected, the FAST calculations will terminate and separate DATING calculations will be
 performed based on the current FAST calculations and the inputs selected.

 The second option is to use the built-in FAST models to specify the time step that the fuel was
 removed from the reactor (see `2KQpHbi2T in 7`T+QM). The advanced boundary conditions can
 then be used to set the axial and time profile for the cladding temperature along with biQTQt,
       //br2HH, and //;KH2b in bT2Mi7m2H.

 els. The bias applied to the models can be performed using either a set number of standard de-
 viations (assuming the standard deviation is known, see the MatLib documentation (Geelhood,
 Luscher, et al. 2026) for more details) or a multiplier on the model’s calculated value.

       B.11     /2p2HQT2` Block
 This block contains parameters that were designed to be used by the code developers to per-
 form targeted code assessments. These inputs can be used to make fundamental changes to the
 models in FAST, and therefore the results when using these options are NOT validated.

 Appendix C – Output and Plotting Capabilities
 This appendix contains the information relating to the output and plotting capabilities of FAST. As
 shown in Table B.1, FAST-1.2.2 can produce an ASCII text-based output file that summarizes the
 results in an easy to read format, as well as an ASCII numerical-only plot file that contains scalars
 and arrays of many important modeling parameters as a function of time. This file was designed to
 work with a Microsoft Excel based tool called FRAPlot as well as AptPlot, which is also designed to
 work with the SNAP tool previously mentioned. However, this file being written in a simple, ASCII
 format will allow it to be read using any text editor.

 C.1 Output File
 The output file is a text-based summary of the calculation results that is laid out in an easy to
 read format. The output file can be broken down into three main sections, as shown in Figure C.1.
 The input processing portion contains the code version used in the analysis, a re-write of the in-
 put file, and a summary report of the fuel rod design and user-supplied coolant conditions and
 power history. The time-dependent results contain important calculated parameters, such as tem-
 perature distributions, dimensional changes, contributions to rod internal pressure, and cladding
 corrosion. These values can be printed for every axial node, the peak power axial node or as an
 axial summary (D/HT`) at the end of every user-specified timestep or as a summary at the end of
 the calculation (MQTi for steady-state calculations, /iTQ for transient calculations). The level of
 detail in the output file is controlled by the input flags.

 Figure C.1. Summary of output file layout

Output and Plotting Capabilities C.1

 C.2 Plot File
 The plot file is an ASCII-based file that contains both scalars and arrays of parameters typically
 assessed against experimental data or other codes. All of the parameters are printed at each
 timestep (as opposed to printing each variable as a function of time at the end of the calculation).
 For steady-state calculations, the print interval is equal to MTHQi, whereas for transient calculations
 it is controlled by /iTHi. The level of detail contained in the plot file is controlled by MTHQi. The
 list of plot variables is shown in Table C.1.

Output and Plotting Capabilities C.2

 C.3 FRAPlot
 The Microsoft Excel-based tool developed for use with the FAST codes is FRAPlot. FRAPlot has a
 series of Macros that enables it to read the plot file, create arrays, develop ListBoxes that contain
 the information about each array, and create plots. There are four Tabs at the bottom of the work-
 book that contain plot-able data which corresponds to each of the boxes from Figure C.2: Data

Output and Plotting Capabilities C.3

 (Scalars), 1D Data (Axial Arrays), 2D Data (2-D (r,z) Arrays) and Gas Data (Gas Arrays).

 Figure C.2. FRAPLot’s tabs containing plottable data

 Note: in order to use FRAPlot, you must 1) have an activated version of Microsoft Excel and 2)
 enable macros when the workbook is opened.

 FRAPlot has the ability to switch between British and SI units, regardless of the units printed in the
 plot file, using the same unit conversions as those contained in FAST. This option is available on
 all Tabs. As shown in Figure C.3, the units selection is in cell B5 and contains a dropdown selection
 of either SI or British.

 Figure C.3. Units selection

 After opening FRAPlot, click the button labeled “Load Plot File”, as shown in Figure C.4. This
 button is available on all four of the Tabs; any of the tabs may be used. When switching from one
 tab to another, this step does not need to be repeated.

Output and Plotting Capabilities C.4

 Figure C.4. “Load Plot File” selection

 A pop-up window will appear that allows you to select the plot file. Click the “Browse” button and
 find your plot file on your computer. Note: This tool will only show files with the XTHQi extension. If
 your plot file was not named with the XTHQi extension, as recommended in Figure C.5, it must be
 renamed 6BH2LK2XTHQi. Once you have selected your plot file, click the “Ok” button.

 Figure C.5. “Load Plot File” pop-up window

 Depending on the size of the plot file, it may take several seconds up to a minute for the file to
 load into Excel’s memory. Once it is loaded, the ListBoxes will populate with the available plotting
 data.

Output and Plotting Capabilities C.5

 Upon
 Loaded
 Opening

 Figure C.6. View of Listboxes once Plot File is loaded into Memory

 To plot the scalar data (Data tab), select a variable from both the X Axis and Y Axis ListBoxes,
 make sure that the proper Units are selected, and press the “Plot” button.

 Figure C.7. Examples of “Data” plot using British units

Output and Plotting Capabilities C.6

 Figure C.8. Examples of “Data” plot using SI units (continued)

 To plot the Axial Arrays, click on the 1D Data tab. There are two plotting buttons on this tab: “Nodal
 Plot” and “Axial Plot”. The “Nodal Plot” button will produce a plot of the selected value for each
 axial node as a function of the X-axis variable selected. The X-axis for the graph in this tab can be
 selected as being either problem time days or rod-average burnup GWd/MTU.

Output and Plotting Capabilities C.7

 Figure C.9. Nodal plot using burnup for x-axis

 The “Axial Plot” button will produce a graph of the selected array versus axial elevation at a certain
 point in time. If the checkbox is not checked where it says “User-specified time for axial plot (default
 last timestep)”, FRAPlot will pull the data at the last timestep. If this checkbox is checked, then the
 code will use the closest timestep that is less than or equal to the value in cell C45, next to the
 words “AXIAL PLOT TIME”. If the value here is larger than the last timestep, it will default to using
 the last timestep. For both plotting options, the user can select the checkbox “Fix Max Extents” if
 they wish to set the minimum and maximum values on the Y-Axis to the minimum and maximum
 values being plotted. An example of the “Axial Plot” is shown in Figure C.10.

Output and Plotting Capabilities C.8

 Figure C.10. Axial Plot with “Fix Max Extents” and “User-specified Time for Axial Plot”

 The 2D Data plotting options are similar to the 1D Data options, without the ability to select the
 X-axis variables. Rather than having an X- and Y-axis, there is a Horizontal (similar to X-axis),
 Vertical (similar to Y-axis) and a Depth axis, as shown in Figure C.10. The Horizontal axis is for the
 radial nodes for the entire fuel rod (fuel, gap, clad, oxide, crud), where 1 represents the outermost
 (cladding OD crud) temperature and each incremental number increase moves inward by 1 mesh
 point towards the fuel centerline. The Depth axis is for the axial nodes, where 1 corresponds to
 the bottom-most segment of the fuel rod.

Output and Plotting Capabilities C.9

 Figure C.11. Example of a 2D Data plot

 The Gas Data tab will only contain data if the proper value is used in the input variable MTHQi. This
 tab is very similar to the 2D Data tab, with the plotting button labeled as “Gas Plot”. However, the
 radial gas nodes are for the fuel mesh only and use a reversed nodalization from the temperature
 calculation (where 1 corresponds to the fuel centerline).

Output and Plotting Capabilities C.10

 Figure C.12. Example of a Gas Data plot

 C.4 AptPlot
 The ACS plug-in has been updated for use with AptPlot that allows it to read the plot file gener-
 ated by FAST. This section is a brief overview of how to load the FRAPCON file into AptPlot. For
 more detailed information regarding the capabilities of AptPlot, visit https://www.aptplot.com. Ad-
 ditionally, AptPlot contains a “Help” feature that contains additional information regarding the ACS
 plug-in and creating FAST plots.

 The same options that were outlined in Table C.1 are available for plotting. After opening up the
 AptPlot program, search for the FAST-generated plot file by selecting File -> Read -> FAST data. A
 pop-up window will appear that will allow for the selection of the 6AG1LJ1XTHQi file. After selecting
 it, press Open. These steps are shown in Figure C.13 and C.14.

Output and Plotting Capabilities C.11

 Figure C.13. How to load FAST plot file in AptPlot (a)

 Figure C.14. How to load FAST plot file in AptPlot (b)

 After pressing Open, a new window will appear (Figure C.15) that contains all of the selectable
 variables (these are referred to as data channels within AptPlot). In this window, there are several
 options similar to those available in the FRAPlot tool. The user can select the plotting units (SI/Bri-

Output and Plotting Capabilities C.12

 tish) and X-axis Time units. Rather than the multiple Tabs in the FRAPlot tool, a drop-down menu
 is available that allows for multiple Plot Types: Time Dependent, Axial and Radial plots. For the
 Axial and Radial plots, the plot time can be input; otherwise, it will default to the last time step.

 Figure C.15. Available data channels for the FAST plug-in in AptPlot

 After selecting the data channel(s), the units, and plot time (if available), press plot. If FAST was
 renodalized during the problem, results shown through AptPlot may be missing information be-
 cause changes have not been made to AptPlot yet to account for renodalization. Examples of
 Time Dependent and Axial plots are shown in Figure C.16.

 The superscripts m and m+1 represent the values of quantities at the old (m) and new (m+1) time.
 The steady-state finite difference equations are obtained by setting the left side of Equations D.1
 through D.5 to zero and by dropping the superscripts m and m + 1. Equations D.1 through D.5 can
 be written in the following simplified form by combining constant coefficients and known tempera-
 tures (Tjm ).

 Equations D.7 through D.14 represent a set of six equations having six unknowns.

 In the above equations, all material properties and heat transfer coefficients (except convection to
 the coolant) are shown as constants. For the transient case, the temperature-dependent material
 properties and heat transfer coefficients are evaluated at the average of the temperatures (TBAR)
 at the start and end times of each time step. For the steady-state calculation, TBAR represents an
 estimate of the true steady-state temperature. Therefore, it is required that the steady-state and
 transient solutions to Equations D.7 through D.14 be iterated to convergence on TBAR.

 Appendix E – Lua Scripting in FAST
 FAST now supports lua scripting, allowing users to override many material properties by adding
 custom scripts to the input file.

 Using custom lua material properties invalidates the verification and validation testing that is per-
 formed on FAST. Proceed with caution!

 E.1 Lua Details
 Lua version 5.1 is embedded in FAST. For information on the lua scripting language, visit lua.org.

 The full lua scripting language is available, and code not contained in a function will be executed
 during the input phase of the calculation. For example, this can be used to open files for dumping
 debug information. Code can also be in additional files with the XHm suffix and included using the
 `2[mB`2 statement:

 `2[mB`2 lPkXHm

 Comments are allowed and are denoted by double hyphens:

 @@i?Bb Bb  Hm +QKK2Mi

 Lua custom material property examples are included with the FAST executable distribution pack-
 age.

 E.2 Overriding Material Properties
 The input file must still select valid material types. The built-in functions will be used for any prop-
 erties not overridden by the user.

 To override a function, the user must:

 1. Include a line with only

 OGl

 after all of the normal FAST input.

 2. Include one or more properly named functions after the OGl line, or in files imported by a
 require statement.

 Valid property function names can be determined by combining the material type and property
 names found in Table E.1. For example, a function named *H/tBH:`Qri? or 6m2H1Mi?HTv, will
 override their respective MatLib function.

Lua Scripting in FAST E.1

 Typos in names will generally not be caught as an error, as the user can create helper functions
 with other names.

 All inputs to the function will be provided in a single input parameter that takes the form of a library
 data structure. This is named by the user in the function definition, but in all provided examples
 the name “params” is used. Inputs can then be accessed with the syntax T`KbXp`B#H2MK2,
 where p`B#H2MK2 is one of the inputs provided by FAST for the given function.

Lua Scripting in FAST E.2

Lua Scripting in FAST E.3

 E.3 Output Warnings
 If any lua properties are included, the FAST output file will contain a warning that the results are
 not validated and a list of functions that are overridden by custom lua scripts.

 E.4 Convenience Features
 In addition to any property-specific input parameters, the following are always available in the
 dictionary:

 Table E.2. Always-Available Functions

 Variable Definition Units
 it Timestep Unitless
 Time Problem time Seconds
 Result Result from MatLib calculation Varies
 Z* Axial node Unitless
 * Z is not available for Fluid, Gas, or Water

 A convenience function is provided which can be used to print the input parameter library for a
 given function. A user may call:

 b?QrS`KbUT`Kb(- 7BH2)V

 where T`Kb is the full dictionary passed to a validly named material property function, and 7BH2
 is an optional open file handle.

 This function will output a template for the given function to the file QmiTmiMK2XHQmi, where
 QmiTmiMK2 is the name of the XQmi file created by FAST. This template includes descriptions
 of the output and all inputs, including units, in a format that can be cut and paste into the input file
 for further modification. This function can be called from any number of property functions and will
 print each template once.

 E.5 Troubleshooting
 If the lua code has a syntax error, FAST will exit with an error during input processing and write
 the error to the screen and the output file.

 E.6 Suggested Workflow
 Create the FAST input, then open the file in a text editor. At the bottom of the file, add a line that
 contains only:

 OGl

Lua Scripting in FAST E.4

 Below this line, include the following function for each property that will be modified (replacing
 *H/uQmM;JQ/ with the desired material/property name from Table E.1):

 7mM+iBQM *H/uQmM;JQ/UT`KbV
 b?QrS`KbUT`KbV @@ T`BMi 7mM+iBQM i2KTHi2
           `2im`M T`KbX_2bmHi @@ `2im`M `2bmHi T`QpB/2/ #v JiHB#
 2M/

 After adding the function(s), save and run the input file.

 Next open the XQmi file generated by the run. Scroll to the bottom, and inspect the last few lines
 for errors that would indicate that you have a typo. For example, you might see something like:

       2``Q` HQ/BM; 7BH2

       (bi`BM; ]HQ+H Q64Ubi`BM;XKi+?U]GlnwB`+9nrBi?ni`MXXX]),R, (bi`BM;
 ]GlnwB`+9nrBi?ni`MbB2MinHmXBM]),jye, mM2tT2+i2/ bvK#QH M2` ^2M/^

 This would indicate that you have an issue in your input file on (or adjacent to) line 306, near the
 word “end.” Correct any errors and repeat until the file runs.

 Now, ensure that the lua functions were named correctly. Check the XQmi file for the warning:

 Gm Ki2`BH T`QT2`iv b+`BTiBM; QTiBQMb mb2/X

 LPh1, h>1 la1 P6 h>1a1 PShAPLa Ju ALoGA.h1 h>1 _1alGha P6 h>1
 *G*lGhAPLX h>1 6ah oGA.hAPL h1ah *a1a .P LPh la1 h>1a1
 PShAPLaX la1 h uPl_ PqL .Aa*_1hAPLX

        h?2 7QHHQrBM; T`QT2`iB2b ?p2 #22M Qp2``B//2M #v i?2 mb2`,

 Examine the list of properties that follows to ensure that the lua functions are named correctly. Lua
 is case-sensitive, so ensure that capitalization is consistent with Table E.1.

 Next, check the directory where the file was run, and open the XHQmi file. This should now contain
 function templates with available inputs and expected return type, complete with comments and
 units. Copy the contents of the file into the input file, replacing everything below the OGl line. Write
 your functions as desired, checking the end of the XQmi file for errors returned by lua.

 If the XHQmi file is blank, or if it is missing one or more functions, then the missing functions were
 not called by FAST. This may be due to a typo in the function name, an error in the lua script or
 FAST run that prematurely terminated the calculation (check the XQmi file), or it may simply be a
 consequence of the way the calculation progressed.

Lua Scripting in FAST E.5

 For further troubleshooting, the XHQmi file can be accessed using the file handle Qmi6BH2. For
 example, to print an intermediate variable named t to the file the following could be added to the
 function:

 Qmi6BH2,r`Bi2U]h?2 pHm2 Q7 t i iBK2 ] XX T`KbXhBK2 XX ] Bb ] XX tV

 Additional files may also be opened using standard lua practices if needed. If FAST exits with a
 fortran exception, which can sometimes happen if a lua function returns a value wildly outside
 what FAST expects (e.g., negative or zero values), lua may not write the file out if the buffer isn’t
 flushed. If this is encountered, add Qmi6BH2,7Hmb?UV after the r`Bi2UV command (substitute the
 file name if needed).

Lua Scripting in FAST E.6
